2x" data-file-width="5672" data-file-height="3545" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%A1%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%BA%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%8F_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%BF%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%82%D1%8C_Cetatea_Soroca_Soroca_Fortress_(44738806701).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Built during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Stephen_III_of_Moldavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen III of Moldavia">Stephen the Great</a>, several authors believed the <a href="/wiki/Soroca_Fort" title="Soroca Fort">Soroca Fort</a> was constructed on the site of a former <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">Genoese</a> fortress named <i>Olhionia</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Moldova_(1483)-en.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Moldova_%281483%29-en.png/220px-Moldova_%281483%29-en.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="201" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Moldova_%281483%29-en.png/330px-Moldova_%281483%29-en.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Moldova_%281483%29-en.png/440px-Moldova_%281483%29-en.png 2x" data-file-width="1769" data-file-height="1614" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Moldova_(1483)-en.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Administrative map of the Principality of Moldavia in 1483, with surrounding states</div></div></div>
<p>For all of his success, it was under the reign of Alexander I that the first confrontation with the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Turks" title="Ottoman Turks">Ottoman Turks</a> took place at Cetatea Albă in 1420. A deep crisis was to follow Alexander l's long reign, with his successors battling each other in a succession of wars that divided the country until the murder of <a href="/wiki/Bogdan_II_of_Moldavia" title="Bogdan II of Moldavia">Bogdan II</a> and the accession of <a href="/wiki/Peter_Aaron" title="Peter Aaron">Peter Aaron</a> in 1451. Nevertheless, Moldavia was subject to further Hungarian interventions after that moment, as <a href="/wiki/Matthias_Corvinus" title="Matthias Corvinus">Matthias Corvinus</a> deposed Aaron and backed <a href="/wiki/Alex%C4%83ndrel" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexăndrel">Alexăndrel</a> to the throne in <a href="/wiki/Suceava" title="Suceava">Suceava</a>. Peter Aaron's rule also signified the beginning of Moldavia's <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> allegiance, as the ruler was the first to agree to pay <a href="/wiki/Tribute" title="Tribute">tribute</a> to Sultan <a href="/wiki/Mehmed_II" title="Mehmed II">Mehmed II</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Moldavia_at_its_apogee">Moldavia at its apogee</span></h4>
<p>Peter Aaron was eventually ousted by his nephew, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_the_Great" title="Stephen the Great">Stephen the Great</a> who would become the most important medieval Moldavian ruler who managed to uphold Moldavia's autonomy against Hungary, Poland and the Ottoman Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-Sedlar1994_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sedlar1994-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Pop1999_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pop1999-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Under his rule, which lasted 47 years, Moldavia experienced a glorious political and cultural period.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Age_of_Invasions">Age of Invasions</span></h4>
<p>During this time, Moldavia was invaded repeatedly by <a href="/wiki/Crimean_Tatars" title="Crimean Tatars">Crimean Tatars</a> and, beginning in the 15th century, by the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Turks</a>. In 1538, the principality became a <a href="/wiki/Tribute" title="Tribute">tributary</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, but it retained internal and partial external autonomy.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, the <a href="/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth" title="Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth">Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth</a> continued to strongly influence Moldavia both through national politics as well as on the local level through significant intermarriage between Moldavian nobility and the Polish <a href="/wiki/Szlachta" title="Szlachta">szlachta</a>. When in May 1600, <a href="/wiki/Michael_the_Brave" title="Michael the Brave">Michael the Brave</a> removed <a href="/wiki/Ieremia_Movil%C4%83" title="Ieremia Movilă">Ieremia Movilă</a> from Moldavia's throne by winning the battle of <a href="/wiki/Bac%C4%83u" title="Bacău">Bacău</a>, briefly reuniting under his rule <a href="/wiki/Moldavia" title="Moldavia">Moldavia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wallachia" title="Wallachia">Wallachia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Transylvania" title="Transylvania">Transylvania</a>, a Polish army led by <a href="/wiki/Jan_Zamoyski" title="Jan Zamoyski">Jan Zamoyski</a> drove the Wallachians from Moldavia. Zamoyski reinstalled Ieremia Movilă to the throne, who put the country under the vassalage of the <a href="/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth" title="Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth">Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth</a>. Moldavia finally returned to Ottoman vassalage in 1621.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Transnistria">Transnistria</span></h4>
<p>While the region of <a href="/wiki/Transnistria_(geographical_region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Transnistria (geographical region)">Transnistria</a> was never politically part of the Principality of Moldavia, there were sizable areas which were owned by Moldavian boyars or the Moldavian rulers. The earliest surviving deeds referring to lands beyond the Dniester river date from the 16th century.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#What_information_to_include" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="A complete citation is needed. (May 2021)">full citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Moldavian chronicler <a href="/wiki/Grigore_Ureche" title="Grigore Ureche">Grigore Ureche</a> mentions that in 1584 some Moldavian villages from beyond the Dniester in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland" title="Kingdom of Poland">Kingdom of Poland</a> were attacked and plundered by <a href="/wiki/Cossacks" title="Cossacks">Cossacks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:No_original_research#Primary,_secondary_and_tertiary_sources" title="Wikipedia:No original research"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable secondary sources. (May 2021)">non-primary source needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>  Many Moldavians were members of Cossacks units, with two of them, <a href="/wiki/Ioan_Potcoav%C4%83" class="mw-redirect" title="Ioan Potcoavă">Ioan Potcoavă</a> and <a href="/wiki/Danylo_Apostol" title="Danylo Apostol">Dănilă Apostol</a> becoming <a href="/wiki/Hetman" title="Hetman">hetmans</a> of Ukraine. Ruxandra Lupu, the daughter of Moldavian voivode <a href="/wiki/Vasile_Lupu" title="Vasile Lupu">Vasile Lupu</a> who married <a href="/w/index.php?title=Tymish_Khmelnytsky&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Tymish Khmelnytsky (page does not exist)">Tymish Khmelnytsky</a>, lived in <a href="/wiki/Ra%C8%99cov" title="Rașcov">Rașcov</a> according to Ukrainian tradition.
</p><p>While most of today's Moldova came into the Ottoman orbit in the 16th century, a substantial part of Transnistria remained a part of the <a href="/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth" title="Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth">Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth</a> until the <a href="/wiki/Second_Partition_of_Poland" title="Second Partition of Poland">Second Partition of Poland</a> in 1793.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Russian_Empire">Russian Empire</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:262px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png/260px-Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png" decoding="async" width="260" height="198" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png/390px-Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/ed/Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png 2x" data-file-width="464" data-file-height="354" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Treaty_of_Bucharest-1812.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Territorial changes of Moldavia following the Treaty of Bucharest 1812.</div></div></div>
<p>In accordance with the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Bucharest,_1812" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Bucharest, 1812">Treaty of Bucharest of 1812</a>, and despite numerous protests by Moldavian nobles on behalf of the sovereignty of their principality, the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> (of which Moldavia was a <a href="/wiki/Vassal_state" title="Vassal state">vassal</a>) ceded to the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> the <a href="/wiki/Bessarabia" title="Bessarabia">eastern half of the territory</a> of the Principality of Moldavia along with <a href="/wiki/Khotyn" title="Khotyn">Khotyn</a> and old Bessarabia (modern <a href="/wiki/Budjak" title="Budjak">Budjak</a>), which Russia had already conquered and annexed. The new Russian province was called <a href="/wiki/Bessarabia_Governorate" title="Bessarabia Governorate">Oblast of Moldavia and Bessarabia</a>, and initially enjoyed a large degree of autonomy. After 1828 this autonomy was progressively restricted and in 1871 the Oblast was transformed into the Bessarabia Governorate, in a process of <a href="/wiki/Forced_assimilation" title="Forced assimilation">state-imposed assimilation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russification" title="Russification">Russification</a>. As part of this process, the <a href="/wiki/Tsarist" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsarist">Tsarist</a> administration in Bessarabia gradually removed the <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Moldavian language">Moldavian language</a> from official and religious use.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Union_with_Romania_and_the_return_of_the_Russians">Union with Romania and the return of the Russians</span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1856)" title="Treaty of Paris (1856)">Treaty of Paris (1856)</a> returned the <a href="/wiki/Southern_Bessarabia" title="Southern Bessarabia">southern part of Bessarabia</a> (later organised as the <a href="/wiki/Cahul_County" title="Cahul County">Cahul</a>, Bolgrad and Ismail counties) to Moldavia, which remained an autonomous principality and, in 1859, <a href="/wiki/Unification_of_Moldavia_and_Wallachia" title="Unification of Moldavia and Wallachia">united</a> with <a href="/wiki/Wallachia" title="Wallachia">Wallachia</a> to form <a href="/wiki/United_Principalities" class="mw-redirect" title="United Principalities">Romania</a>. In 1878, as a result of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Berlin_(1878)" title="Treaty of Berlin (1878)">Treaty of Berlin</a>, Romania was forced to cede the three counties back to the Russian Empire.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="A_multiethnic_colonization">A multiethnic colonization</span></h4>
<p>Over the 19th century, the Russian authorities encouraged the <a href="/wiki/Colonization" title="Colonization">colonization</a> of Bessarabia or parts of it by <a href="/wiki/Russians_in_Moldova" title="Russians in Moldova">Russians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ukrainians" title="Ukrainians">Ukrainians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bessarabian_Germans" class="mw-redirect" title="Bessarabian Germans">Germans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bessarabian_Bulgarians" title="Bessarabian Bulgarians">Bulgarians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poles_in_Moldova" title="Poles in Moldova">Poles</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gagauzes" class="mw-redirect" title="Gagauzes">Gagauzes</a>, primarily in the northern and southern areas vacated by Turks and <a href="/wiki/Nogais" title="Nogais">Nogais</a>, the latter having been expelled in the 1770s and 1780s, during the <a href="/wiki/Russo-Turkish_Wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Russo-Turkish Wars">Russo-Turkish Wars</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-washington_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-washington-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> the inclusion of the province in the <a href="/wiki/Pale_of_Settlement" title="Pale of Settlement">Pale of Settlement</a> also allowed the immigration of more <a href="/wiki/Bessarabian_Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="Bessarabian Jews">Bessarabian Jews</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fn1_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fn1-46">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> The Moldavian proportion of the population decreased from an estimated 86% in 1816,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> to around 52% in 1905.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> During this time there were anti-Semitic riots, leading to an exodus of thousands of Jews to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:282px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Greater_Romania.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Greater_Romania.svg/280px-Greater_Romania.svg.png" decoding="async" width="280" height="204" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Greater_Romania.svg/420px-Greater_Romania.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Greater_Romania.svg/560px-Greater_Romania.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1744" data-file-height="1270" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Greater_Romania.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A map of <a href="/wiki/Greater_Romania" title="Greater Romania">Greater Romania</a> between 1920 and 1940.</div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Russian_Revolution">Russian Revolution</span></h4>
<p><a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> brought in a rise in political and cultural (ethnic) awareness among the inhabitants of the region, as 300,000 Bessarabians were drafted into the <a href="/wiki/Red_Army" title="Red Army">Russian Army</a> formed in 1917; within bigger units several "Moldavian Soldiers' Committees" were formed. Following the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Revolution_(1917)" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Revolution (1917)">Russian Revolution of 1917</a>, a Bessarabian parliament, <a href="/wiki/Sfatul_%C8%9A%C4%83rii" title="Sfatul Țării">Sfatul Țării</a> (a National Council), was <a href="/wiki/1917_Sfatul_%C8%9A%C4%83rii_election" title="1917 Sfatul Țării election">elected in October–November 1917</a> and opened on December 3&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 21 November&#93;&#160;1917. The Sfatul Țării proclaimed the <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Democratic_Republic" title="Moldavian Democratic Republic">Moldavian Democratic Republic</a> (December 15&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 2 December&#93;&#160;1917) within a federal Russian state, and formed a <a href="/wiki/Pantelimon_Erhan_Cabinet" title="Pantelimon Erhan Cabinet">government</a> (21 December&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 8 December&#93;&#160;1917).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Greater_Romania">Greater Romania</span></h3>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Romanian_army" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanian army">Romanian army</a> <a href="/wiki/Romanian_military_intervention_in_Bessarabia" title="Romanian military intervention in Bessarabia">occupied the region</a> in early January 1918 at the request of the National Council, Bessarabia proclaimed independence from Russia on February 6&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 24 January&#93;&#160;1918 and requested the assistance of the French army present in Romania (general <a href="/wiki/Henri_Mathias_Berthelot" title="Henri Mathias Berthelot">Henri Berthelot</a>) and of the Romanian Army.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> On April 9&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 27 March&#93;&#160;1918, the Sfatul Țării decided with 86 votes for, 3 against and 36 abstaining, to <a href="/wiki/Union_of_Bessarabia_with_Romania" title="Union of Bessarabia with Romania">unite with the Kingdom of Romania</a>. The union was conditional upon fulfilment of the <a href="/wiki/Land_reform_in_Romania,_1921" class="mw-redirect" title="Land reform in Romania, 1921">agrarian reform</a>, autonomy, and respect for universal human rights.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> A part of the interim Parliament agreed to drop these conditions after <a href="/wiki/Bukovina" title="Bukovina">Bukovina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Transylvania" title="Transylvania">Transylvania</a> also joined the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania" title="Kingdom of Romania">Kingdom of Romania</a>, although historians note that they lacked the <a href="/wiki/Quorum" title="Quorum">quorum</a> to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This union was recognized by most of the principal <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allied Powers</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1920)" title="Treaty of Paris (1920)">1920 Treaty of Paris</a>, which however was not ratified by all of its signatories.<sup id="cite_ref-legal_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-legal-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/RSFSR" class="mw-redirect" title="RSFSR">newly Soviet Russia</a> did not recognize Romanian rule over Bessarabia, considering it an occupation of Russian territory.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Uprisings against Romanian rule took place in 1919 at <a href="/wiki/Khotyn_Uprising" title="Khotyn Uprising">Khotyn</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bender_Uprising" title="Bender Uprising">Bender</a>, but were eventually suppressed by the Romanian Army.
</p><p>In May 1919, the <a href="/wiki/Bessarabian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Bessarabian Soviet Socialist Republic">Bessarabian Soviet Socialist Republic</a> was proclaimed as a government in exile. After the failure of the <a href="/wiki/Tatarbunary_Uprising" title="Tatarbunary Uprising">Tatarbunary Uprising</a> in 1924, the Moldavian Autonomous Region, created earlier in the Transnistria region, was elevated to an <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Moldavian Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic">Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic</a> within the <a href="/wiki/Ukrainian_SSR" class="mw-redirect" title="Ukrainian SSR">Ukrainian SSR</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="World_War_II_and_Soviet_era">World War II and Soviet era</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic">Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II,_the_village_Cojusna,_Straseni_District%22_(80-ies_famous)._(6177439670).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II%2C_the_village_Cojusna%2C_Straseni_District%22_%2880-ies_famous%29._%286177439670%29.jpg/220px-%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II%2C_the_village_Cojusna%2C_Straseni_District%22_%2880-ies_famous%29._%286177439670%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II%2C_the_village_Cojusna%2C_Straseni_District%22_%2880-ies_famous%29._%286177439670%29.jpg/330px-%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II%2C_the_village_Cojusna%2C_Straseni_District%22_%2880-ies_famous%29._%286177439670%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II%2C_the_village_Cojusna%2C_Straseni_District%22_%2880-ies_famous%29._%286177439670%29.jpg/440px-%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II%2C_the_village_Cojusna%2C_Straseni_District%22_%2880-ies_famous%29._%286177439670%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="513" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:%22Monument_to_the_villagers_who_died_in_World_War_II,_the_village_Cojusna,_Straseni_District%22_(80-ies_famous)._(6177439670).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Monument to the villagers who died in <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, the village <a href="/wiki/Coju%C8%99na" title="Cojușna">Cojușna</a>, <a href="/wiki/Str%C4%83%C8%99eni_District" title="Strășeni District">Strășeni District</a>.</div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Annexation_by_the_USSR">Annexation by the USSR</span></h4>
<p>In August 1939, the <a href="/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact" title="Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact">Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact</a> and its secret additional protocol were signed, by which <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a> recognized Bessarabia as being within the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_sphere_of_influence" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviet sphere of influence">Soviet sphere of influence</a>, which led the latter to actively revive its claim to the region.<sup id="cite_ref-Olson_1994_483_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Olson_1994_483-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> On 28 June 1940, the Soviet Union issued an <a href="/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Bessarabia_and_Northern_Bukovina" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviet occupation of Bessarabia and Northern Bukovina">ultimatum to Romania</a> requesting the cession of Bessarabia and Northern Bukovina, with which Romania complied the following day. Soon after, the <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic">Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic</a> (Moldavian SSR, MSSR) was established,<sup id="cite_ref-Olson_1994_483_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Olson_1994_483-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> comprising about 65% of Bessarabia, and 50% of the now-disbanded Moldavian ASSR (the present-day <a href="/wiki/Transnistria" title="Transnistria">Transnistria</a>). Ethnic Germans <a href="/wiki/Heim_ins_Reich" title="Heim ins Reich">left</a> in 1940.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Reincorporation_into_Romania_and_the_Soviet_occupation">Reincorporation into Romania and the Soviet occupation</span></h4>
<p>As part of the 1941 <a href="/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa" title="Operation Barbarossa">Axis invasion of the Soviet Union</a>, Romania regained the territories of Bessarabia and Northern Bukovina, and seized a territory which became known as <a href="/wiki/Transnistria_Governorate" title="Transnistria Governorate">Transnistria Governorate</a>. Romanian forces, working with the Germans, <a href="/wiki/Ion_Antonescu#Antonescu_and_the_Holocaust" title="Ion Antonescu">deported or massacred</a> about 300,000 Jews<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>, including 147,000 from Bessarabia and Bukovina. Of the latter, approximately 90,000 died.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Between 1941 and 1944 <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_resistance_during_World_War_II" title="Moldovan resistance during World War II">partisan detachments</a> acted against the Romanian administration. The Soviet Army re-captured the region in February–August 1944, and re-established the Moldavian SSR. Between the end of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Jassy%E2%80%93Kishinev_Offensive" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Jassy–Kishinev Offensive">Second Jassy–Kishinev Offensive</a> in August 1944 and the end of the war in May 1945, 256,800 inhabitants of the Moldavian SSR were drafted into the Soviet Army. 40,592 of them perished.<sup id="cite_ref-history_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-history-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360,_Bessarabien,_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360%2C_Bessarabien%2C_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg/220px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360%2C_Bessarabien%2C_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360%2C_Bessarabien%2C_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg/330px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360%2C_Bessarabien%2C_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360%2C_Bessarabien%2C_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg/440px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360%2C_Bessarabien%2C_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg 2x" data-file-width="798" data-file-height="534" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-065360,_Bessarabien,_Abtransport_von_Umsiedlern.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Bessarabia_Germans" title="Bessarabia Germans">Bessarabia Germans</a> <a href="/wiki/Heim_ins_Reich" title="Heim ins Reich">evacuating</a> after the Soviet occupation of <a href="/wiki/Bessarabia" title="Bessarabia">Bessarabia</a> in 1940.</div></div></div>
<p>During the periods 1940–1941 and 1944–1953, <a href="/wiki/Soviet_deportations_from_Bessarabia_and_Northern_Bukovina" title="Soviet deportations from Bessarabia and Northern Bukovina">deportations of locals</a> to the northern <a href="/wiki/Ural_Mountains" title="Ural Mountains">Urals</a>, to <a href="/wiki/Siberia" title="Siberia">Siberia</a>, and northern <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan" title="Kazakhstan">Kazakhstan</a> occurred regularly, with the largest ones on 12–13 June 1941, and 5–6 July 1949, accounting from MSSR alone for 18,392<sup id="cite_ref-fn2_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fn2-63">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> and 35,796 deportees respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-tismrep_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tismrep-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Other forms of Soviet persecution of the population included political arrests or, in 8,360 cases, execution.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Moldova_in_the_USSR_after_World_War_II">Moldova in the USSR after World War II</span></h4>
<p>In 1946, as a result of a severe drought and excessive delivery quota obligations and requisitions imposed by the Soviet government, the southwestern part of the USSR suffered from a major <a href="/wiki/Droughts_and_famines_in_Russia_and_the_Soviet_Union" title="Droughts and famines in Russia and the Soviet Union">famine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> In 1946–1947, at least 216,000 deaths and about 350,000 cases of <a href="/wiki/Dystrophy" title="Dystrophy">dystrophy</a> were accounted by historians in the Moldavian SSR alone.<sup id="cite_ref-tismrep_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tismrep-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Similar events occurred in the 1930s in the <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_ASSR" class="mw-redirect" title="Moldavian ASSR">Moldavian ASSR</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-tismrep_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tismrep-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> In 1944–53, there were several anti-Soviet resistance groups in Moldova; however the <a href="/wiki/NKVD" title="NKVD">NKVD</a> and later <a href="/wiki/Ministry_for_State_Security_(USSR)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ministry for State Security (USSR)">MGB</a> managed to eventually arrest, execute or deport their members.<sup id="cite_ref-tismrep_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tismrep-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the postwar period, the Soviet government organized the immigration of working age Russian speakers (mostly Russians, <a href="/wiki/Belarusians" title="Belarusians">Belarusians</a>, and Ukrainians), into the new Soviet republic, especially into urbanized areas, partly to compensate for the demographic loss caused by the war and the emigration of 1940 and 1944.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> In the 1970s and 1980s, the Moldavian SSR received substantial allocations from the budget of the USSR to develop industrial and scientific facilities and housing. In 1971, the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_(Soviet_Union)" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Ministers (Soviet Union)">Council of Ministers of the USSR</a> adopted a decision "About the measures for further development of the city of <a href="/wiki/Chi%C8%99in%C4%83u" title="Chișinău">Kishinev</a>" (modern Chișinău), that allotted more than one <a href="/wiki/Billion" title="Billion">billion</a> <a href="/wiki/Soviet_rouble" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviet rouble">roubles</a> (approximately 6.8 billion in 2018 US dollars) from the USSR budget for building projects.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Center_Balti_-_2_(1985)._(18203012941).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Center_Balti_-_2_%281985%29._%2818203012941%29.jpg/200px-Center_Balti_-_2_%281985%29._%2818203012941%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="200" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Center_Balti_-_2_%281985%29._%2818203012941%29.jpg/300px-Center_Balti_-_2_%281985%29._%2818203012941%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Center_Balti_-_2_%281985%29._%2818203012941%29.jpg/400px-Center_Balti_-_2_%281985%29._%2818203012941%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="800" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Center_Balti_-_2_(1985)._(18203012941).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/B%C4%83l%C8%9Bi" title="Bălți">Bălți</a> in <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic">Soviet Moldavia</a> in 1985</div></div></div>
<p>The Soviet government conducted a campaign to promote a Moldovan ethnic identity distinct from that of the Romanians, based on a theory developed during the existence of the <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_ASSR" class="mw-redirect" title="Moldavian ASSR">Moldavian ASSR</a>. Official Soviet policy asserted that the language spoken by <a href="/wiki/Moldovans" title="Moldovans">Moldovans</a> was distinct from the <a href="/wiki/Romanian_language" title="Romanian language">Romanian language</a> (see <a href="/wiki/Moldovenism" title="Moldovenism">Moldovenism</a>). To distinguish the two, during the Soviet period, Moldovan was written in the <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_Cyrillic_alphabet" title="Moldovan Cyrillic alphabet">Cyrillic alphabet</a>, in contrast with Romanian, which since 1860 had been written in the <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Latin alphabet</a>.
</p><p>All independent organizations were severely reprimanded, with the <a href="/wiki/National_Patriotic_Front_(Moldova)" title="National Patriotic Front (Moldova)">National Patriotic Front</a> leaders being sentenced in 1972 to long prison terms.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Commission_for_the_Study_of_the_Communist_Dictatorship_in_Moldova" title="Commission for the Study of the Communist Dictatorship in Moldova">Commission for the Study of the Communist Dictatorship in Moldova</a> is assessing the activity of the communist totalitarian regime.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Glasnost_and_Perestroika">Glasnost and Perestroika</span></h4>
<p>In the 1980s, amid political conditions created by <a href="/wiki/Glasnost" title="Glasnost">glasnost</a> and <a href="/wiki/Perestroika" title="Perestroika">perestroika</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Movement_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Movement of Moldova">Democratic Movement of Moldova</a> was formed, which in 1989 became known as the nationalist <a href="/wiki/Popular_Front_of_Moldova" title="Popular Front of Moldova">Popular Front of Moldova</a> (FPM).<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Along with several other Soviet republics, from 1988 onwards, Moldova started to move towards <a href="/wiki/Independence_of_Moldova" title="Independence of Moldova">independence</a>. On 27 August 1989, the FPM organized a mass demonstration in Chișinău that became known as the <a href="/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly,_Chi%C8%99in%C4%83u_1989" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand National Assembly, Chișinău 1989">Grand National Assembly</a>. The assembly pressured the authorities of the Moldavian SSR to adopt a language law on 31 August 1989 that proclaimed the Moldovan language written in the <a href="/wiki/Latin_script" title="Latin script">Latin script</a> to be the state language of the MSSR. Its identity with the Romanian language was also established.<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lang_law_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_law-72">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> In 1989, as opposition to the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Communist Party of Moldova">Communist Party</a> grew, there were <a href="/wiki/1989_Moldovan_civil_unrest" title="1989 Moldovan civil unrest">major riots in November</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Independence_and_aftermath">Independence and aftermath</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg/170px-Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="222" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg/255px-Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg/340px-Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1005" data-file-height="1311" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gheorghe_Ghimpu_arboreaza_Tricolorul.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Deputy <a href="/wiki/Gheorghe_Ghimpu" title="Gheorghe Ghimpu">Gheorghe Ghimpu</a> replaces the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviet flag">Soviet flag</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_the_Republic_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliament of the Republic of Moldova">Parliament</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Romanian_flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanian flag">Romanian flag</a> on 27 April 1990.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/1990_Moldovan_parliamentary_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1990 Moldovan parliamentary election">first democratic elections</a> for the local <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Moldovan Parliament">parliament</a> were held in February and March 1990. <a href="/wiki/Mircea_Snegur" title="Mircea Snegur">Mircea Snegur</a> was elected as <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Moldovan_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Speaker of the Moldovan Parliament">Speaker of the Parliament</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mircea_Druc" title="Mircea Druc">Mircea Druc</a> as Prime Minister. On 23 June 1990, the Parliament adopted the Declaration of Sovereignty of the "Soviet Socialist Republic Moldova", which, among other things, stipulated the supremacy of Moldovan laws over those of the Soviet Union.<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> After the failure of the <a href="/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_attempt" title="1991 Soviet coup d&#39;état attempt">1991 Soviet coup d'état attempt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Declaration of Independence of Moldova">Moldova declared its independence</a> on 27 August 1991.
</p><p>On 21 December of the same year, Moldova, along with most of the other Soviet republics, signed the constitutive act that formed the post-Soviet <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Independent_States" title="Commonwealth of Independent States">Commonwealth of Independent States</a> (CIS). Moldova received official recognition on 25 December. On 26 December 1991, the Soviet Union ceased to exist. Declaring itself a <a href="/wiki/Neutral_state" class="mw-redirect" title="Neutral state">neutral state</a>, Moldova did not join the military branch of the CIS. Three months later, on 2 March 1992, the country gained formal recognition as an independent state at the United Nations. In 1994, Moldova became a member of <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Partnership_for_Peace" title="Partnership for Peace">Partnership for Peace</a> program, and a member of the Council of Europe on 29 June 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Transnistria_breaks_away_.281990_to_present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Transnistria_breaks_away_(1990_to_present)">Transnistria breaks away (1990 to present)</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Pridnestrovian_Moldavian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Pridnestrovian Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic">Pridnestrovian Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic</a></div>
<p>In the region east of the <a href="/wiki/Dniester" title="Dniester">Dniester</a> river, <a href="/wiki/Transnistria" title="Transnistria">Transnistria</a>, which includes a large proportion of predominantly <a href="/wiki/Russophone" class="mw-redirect" title="Russophone">russophone</a> <a href="/wiki/East_Slavs" title="East Slavs">East Slavs</a> of Ukrainian (28%) and Russian (26%) descent (altogether 54% as of 1989), an independent <a href="/wiki/Pridnestrovian_Moldavian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Pridnestrovian Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic">Pridnestrovian Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic</a> was proclaimed on 16 August 1990, with its capital in <a href="/wiki/Tiraspol" title="Tiraspol">Tiraspol</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> The motives behind this move were fear of the rise of nationalism in Moldova.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In the winter of 1991–1992, clashes occurred between Transnistrian forces, <a href="/wiki/Russian_military_presence_in_Transnistria" title="Russian military presence in Transnistria">supported</a> by elements of the <a href="/wiki/14th_Guards_Army" title="14th Guards Army">14th Guards Army</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Law enforcement in Moldova">Moldovan police</a>. Between 2 March and 26 July 1992, the conflict escalated into a <a href="/wiki/War_of_Transnistria" class="mw-redirect" title="War of Transnistria">military engagement</a>. It was a brief war between Moldovan and separatist Transnistrian forces, with Russia intervening militarily on Transnistria's side. It ended with a ceasefire and the establishment of a security zone policed by a three-way peacekeeping force of Russian, Transnistrian, and Moldovan personnel.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Market_economy_.281992.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Market_economy_(1992)">Market economy (1992)</span></h4>
<p>On 2 January 1992, Moldova introduced a <a href="/wiki/Market_economy" title="Market economy">market economy</a>, liberalizing prices, which resulted in rapid inflation. From 1992 to 2001, the country suffered a serious economic crisis, leaving most of the population below the poverty line. In 1993, the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Moldova" title="Government of Moldova">Government of Moldova</a> introduced a new national currency, the <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_leu" title="Moldovan leu">Moldovan leu</a>, to replace the <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_cupon" title="Moldovan cupon">temporary cupon</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Moldova" title="Economy of Moldova">economy of Moldova</a> began to change in 2001; and until 2008, the country saw a steady annual growth between 5% and 10%. The early 2000s also saw a considerable growth of emigration of Moldovans looking for work (mostly illegally) in Russia (especially the Moscow region), Italy, Portugal, Spain, and other countries; <a href="/wiki/Remittance" title="Remittance">remittances</a> from Moldovans abroad account for almost 38% of Moldova's <a href="/wiki/GDP" class="mw-redirect" title="GDP">GDP</a>, the second-highest percentage in the world, after <a href="/wiki/Tajikistan" title="Tajikistan">Tajikistan</a> (45%).<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Elections:_1994-2009">Elections: 1994-2009</span></h4>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1994_Moldovan_parliamentary_election" title="1994 Moldovan parliamentary election">1994 parliamentary elections</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Agrarian_Party_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Agrarian Party of Moldova">Democratic Agrarian Party</a> gained a majority of the seats, setting a turning point in Moldovan politics. With the nationalist Popular Front now in a parliamentary minority, new measures aiming to moderate the ethnic tensions in the country could be adopted. Plans for a <a href="/wiki/Movement_for_the_unification_of_Romania_and_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Movement for the unification of Romania and Moldova">union with Romania</a> were abandoned,<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> and the new <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Moldova_(1994)" title="Constitution of Moldova (1994)">Constitution</a> gave autonomy to the breakaway Transnistria and Gagauzia. On 23 December 1994, the Parliament of Moldova adopted a "Law on the Special Legal Status of Gagauzia", and in 1995, the latter was constituted.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg/220px-Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg/330px-Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg/440px-Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1329" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Chisinau_riot_2009-04-07_02.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/April_2009_Moldovan_parliamentary_election_protests" title="April 2009 Moldovan parliamentary election protests">Protests</a> outside the <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Moldovan Parliament">Parliament</a> building in 2009</div></div></div>
<p>After winning the <a href="/wiki/1996_Moldovan_presidential_election" title="1996 Moldovan presidential election">1996 presidential elections</a>, on 15 January 1997, <a href="/wiki/Petru_Lucinschi" title="Petru Lucinschi">Petru Lucinschi</a>, the former First Secretary of the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Communist Party of Moldova">Moldavian Communist Party</a> in 1989–91, became the country's second president (1997–2001), succeeding Mircea Snegur (1991–1996). In 2000, the Constitution was amended, transforming Moldova into a <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_republic" title="Parliamentary republic">parliamentary republic</a>, with the president being chosen through <a href="/wiki/Indirect_election" title="Indirect election">indirect election</a> rather than direct popular vote.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/2001_Moldovan_parliamentary_election" title="2001 Moldovan parliamentary election">Winning 49.9% of the vote</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Party_of_Communists_of_the_Republic_of_Moldova" title="Party of Communists of the Republic of Moldova">Party of Communists of the Republic of Moldova</a> (reinstituted in 1993 after being outlawed in 1991), gained 71 of the 101 MPs, and on 4 April 2001, elected <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Voronin" title="Vladimir Voronin">Vladimir Voronin</a> as the country's third president (re-elected in 2005). The country became the first post-Soviet state where a non-reformed Communist Party returned to power.<sup id="cite_ref-lang_matei_70-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lang_matei-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> New governments were formed by <a href="/wiki/Vasile_Tarlev" title="Vasile Tarlev">Vasile Tarlev</a> (19 April 2001 – 31 March 2008), and <a href="/wiki/Zinaida_Grecean%C3%AEi" title="Zinaida Greceanîi">Zinaida Greceanîi</a> (31 March 2008 – 14 September 2009). In 2001–2003, <a href="/wiki/Moldova%E2%80%93Russia_relations" title="Moldova–Russia relations">relations between Moldova and Russia</a> improved, but then temporarily deteriorated in 2003–2006, in the wake of the failure of the <a href="/wiki/Kozak_memorandum" title="Kozak memorandum">Kozak memorandum</a>, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/2006_Russian_ban_of_Moldovan_and_Georgian_wines" title="2006 Russian ban of Moldovan and Georgian wines">2006 wine exports crisis</a>. The Party of Communists of the Republic of Moldova managed to stay in power for eight years.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/April_2009_Moldovan_parliamentary_election" title="April 2009 Moldovan parliamentary election">April 2009 parliamentary elections</a>, the Communist Party won 49.48% of the votes, followed by the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(Moldova)" title="Liberal Party (Moldova)">Liberal Party</a> with 13.14% of the votes, the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Democratic_Party_of_Moldova" title="Liberal Democratic Party of Moldova">Liberal Democratic Party</a> with 12.43%, and the <a href="/wiki/Party_Alliance_Our_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Party Alliance Our Moldova">Alliance "Moldova Noastră"</a> with 9.77%. The controversial results of this election sparked the <a href="/wiki/April_2009_Moldovan_parliamentary_election_protests" title="April 2009 Moldovan parliamentary election protests">April 2009 Moldovan parliamentary election protests</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-omg.md_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-omg.md-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-unimedia.info_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unimedia.info-78">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Stalemate_2009-2012">Stalemate 2009-2012</span></h4>
<p>In August 2009, four Moldovan parties (Liberal Democratic Party, Liberal Party, <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Party of Moldova">Democratic Party</a>, and Our Moldova Alliance) agreed to create the <a href="/wiki/Alliance_For_European_Integration" class="mw-redirect" title="Alliance For European Integration">Alliance For European Integration</a> that pushed the <a href="/wiki/Party_of_Communists_of_the_Republic_of_Moldova" title="Party of Communists of the Republic of Moldova">Party of Communists of the Republic of Moldova</a> into opposition. On 28 August 2009, this coalition chose a new <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Moldovan_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Speaker of the Moldovan Parliament">parliament speaker</a> (<a href="/wiki/Mihai_Ghimpu" title="Mihai Ghimpu">Mihai Ghimpu</a>) in a vote that was boycotted by Communist legislators. <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Voronin" title="Vladimir Voronin">Vladimir Voronin</a>, who had been <a href="/wiki/President_of_Moldova" title="President of Moldova">President of Moldova</a> since 2001, eventually resigned on 11 September 2009, but the <a href="/wiki/Moldovan_presidential_election,_November%E2%80%93December_2009" class="mw-redirect" title="Moldovan presidential election, November–December 2009">Parliament failed to elect a new president</a>. The acting president Mihai Ghimpu instituted the <a href="/wiki/Commission_for_constitutional_reform_in_Moldova" title="Commission for constitutional reform in Moldova">Commission for constitutional reform in Moldova</a> to adopt a new version of the Constitution of Moldova. After the <a href="/wiki/2010_Moldovan_constitutional_referendum" title="2010 Moldovan constitutional referendum">constitutional referendum</a> aimed to approve the reform failed in September 2010,<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> the parliament was dissolved again and a <a href="/wiki/2010_Moldovan_parliamentary_election" title="2010 Moldovan parliamentary election">new parliamentary election</a> was scheduled for 28 November 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> On 30 December 2010, Marian Lupu was elected as the Speaker of the Parliament and the acting President of the Republic of Moldova.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> In March 2012, <a href="/wiki/Nicolae_Timofti" title="Nicolae Timofti">Nicolae Timofti</a> was elected as president of Moldova in a parliamentary vote, becoming the first full-time president since Vladimir Voronin, a Communist, resigned in September 2009. Before the election of Timofti, Moldova had had three acting presidents in three years.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> After the Alliance for European Integration lost a no confidence vote, the <a href="/wiki/Pro-European_Coalition" title="Pro-European Coalition">Pro-European Coalition</a> was formed on 30 May 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Banking_crisis">Banking crisis</span></h4>
<p>In November 2014, Moldova's central bank took control of <i>Banca de Economii</i>, the country's largest lender, and two smaller institutions, <i>Banca Sociala</i> and <i>Unibank</i>. Investigations into activities at these three banks uncovered <a href="/wiki/2014_Moldovan_bank_fraud_scandal" title="2014 Moldovan bank fraud scandal">large-scale fraud</a> by means of fraudulent loans to business entities controlled by a Moldovan-Israeli <a href="/wiki/Business_oligarch" title="Business oligarch">business oligarch</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ilan_Shor" title="Ilan Shor">Ilan Shor</a>, of funds worth about 1 billion U.S. dollars.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The large scale of the fraud compared to the size of the Moldovan economy is cited as tilting the country's politics in favour of the pro-Russian <a href="/wiki/Party_of_Socialists_of_the_Republic_of_Moldova" title="Party of Socialists of the Republic of Moldova">Party of Socialists of the Republic of Moldova</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In 2015, Shor was still at large, after a period of house arrest.
</p>
<h4><span id="Pavel_Filip.27s_government_.282016-2019.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Pavel_Filip's_government_(2016-2019)">Pavel Filip's government (2016-2019)</span></h4>
<p>Following a period of political instability and massive public protests, a new government led by <a href="/wiki/Pavel_Filip" title="Pavel Filip">Pavel Filip</a> was invested in January 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-theguardian.com_2016-02-16_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theguardian.com_2016-02-16-86">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Concerns over statewide corruption, the independence of the judiciary system, and the nontransparency of the banking system were expressed. Germany's broadcaster <a href="/wiki/Deutsche_Welle" title="Deutsche Welle">Deutsche Welle</a> also raised concerns about the alleged influence of Moldovan oligarch <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Plahotniuc" title="Vladimir Plahotniuc">Vladimir Plahotniuc</a> over the Filip government.<sup id="cite_ref-Romania_Curata_Deutsche_Welle_2016-02-16_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Romania_Curata_Deutsche_Welle_2016-02-16-87">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/2016_Moldovan_presidential_election" title="2016 Moldovan presidential election">December 2016 presidential election</a>, Socialist, pro-Russian <a href="/wiki/Igor_Dodon" title="Igor Dodon">Igor Dodon</a> was elected as the new president of the republic.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2019_constitutional_crisis">2019 constitutional crisis</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2019_Moldovan_constitutional_crisis" title="2019 Moldovan constitutional crisis">2019 Moldovan constitutional crisis</a></div>
<p>In 2019, from 7 to 15 June, the Moldovan government went through a period of dual power in what is known as the <a href="/wiki/2019_Moldovan_constitutional_crisis" title="2019 Moldovan constitutional crisis">2019 Moldovan constitutional crisis</a>. On 7 June, the Constitutional Court, which is largely believed to be controlled by Vladimir Plahotniuc<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> from the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Moldova" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Party of Moldova">Democratic Party</a>, announced that they had temporarily removed the sitting president, Igor Dodon, from power due to his ‘inability’ to call new parliamentary elections as the parliament did not form a coalition within three months of the validation of the election results. According to Moldovan constitutional law, the president may call snap elections if no government is formed after three months.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> However, on 8 June, the <a href="/wiki/NOW_Platform_DA_and_PAS" title="NOW Platform DA and PAS">NOW Platform DA and PAS</a> reached an agreement with the <a href="/wiki/Party_of_Socialists_of_the_Republic_of_Moldova" title="Party of Socialists of the Republic of Moldova">Socialist party</a> forming a government led by Maia Sandu as the new prime minister, pushing the Democratic Party out of power.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> This new government was also supported by Igor Dodon. The new coalition and Igor Dodon argued that the president may call snap elections after consulting the parliament but is not obliged to do so. Additionally, because the election results were verified on 9 March, three months should be interpreted as three calendar months, not 90 days as was the case. The former prime minister, Pavel Filip from the Democratic Party, said that new parliamentary elections would be held on 6 September and refused to recognize the new coalition, calling it an illegal government. After a week of dual government meetings, some protest, and the international community mostly supporting the new government coalition, Pavel Filip stepped down as prime minister but still called for new elections.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The Constitutional Court reversed the decision on 15 June, effectively ending the crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="COVID-19_pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</span></h4>
<p>In March 2020, due to the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</a>, the government called a "national red code alert" as the number of coronavirus cases in the country rose to six on 13 March 2020. Government "banned all gatherings of over 50 people until 1 April 2020 and closed all schools and kindergartens in an attempt to curb the spread of the virus". Flights were banned to Spain, Italy, France, Austria, Belgium, Czech Republic, Cyprus, Germany, Ireland, the U.K., Poland, Portugal and Romania.<sup id="cite_ref-sn1_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sn1-94">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> On 17 March, Parliament declared a <a href="/wiki/State_of_emergency" title="State of emergency">state of emergency</a> for at least 60 days, suspended all international flights and closed borders with neighbours Romania and Ukraine. Moldova reported 29 cases of the disease on 17 March 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-sn2_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sn2-95">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The country reported its first death from the disease on 18 March 2020, when the total number of cases reached 30.<sup id="cite_ref-atr_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-atr-96">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_of_Maia_Sandu_since_2020">Presidency of Maia Sandu since 2020</span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti
